Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We need to find the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). 

First, I recall that the standard form of a parabola depends on whether it opens upwards, downwards, to the left, or to the right. Since the vertex is at the origin (0, 0), our equation will be simpler.

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), this indicates that the parabola opens upwards because the focus is above the vertex.

For a parabola that opens upward with vertex at (h, k) = (0, 0), the standard form of the equation is:

\[ y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \]

Here, \( p \) represents the distance from the vertex to the focus. In this case, since the focus is at (0, 4), we have:

\[ p = 4 \]

Now, substitute \( p \) into the equation:

\[ y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 \]
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

So, the equation of the parabola is:

\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]
</think>

To find the equation of a parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the direction of the parabola**:  
   Since the focus is above the vertex, the parabola opens upward.

2. **Use the standard form for an upward-opening parabola with vertex at the origin**:  
   \[ y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \]
   where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

3. **Substitute the value of \( p \)**:  
   Here, \( p = 4 \).

\[ y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 \]
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

**Final Answer**:  
\boxed{y = \frac{1}{16}x^2}

Alternative Approach:

To determine how many parabolas have the line \( y = 2 \) as their directrix and pass through the point \( (3, 4) \), we start by using the definition of a parabola: the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point (the focus) and a fixed line (the directrix).

Given the directrix \( y = 2 \) and a point \( (3, 4) \) on the parabola, we need to find the possible foci. The distance from any point on the parabola to the focus must equal its distance to the directrix.

For the point \( (3, 4) \), let the focus be \( (h, k) \). The distance from \( (3, 4) \) to the focus is:
\[
\sqrt{(h - 3)^2 + (k - 4)^2}
\]
The distance from \( (3, 4) \) to the directrix \( y = 2 \) is:
\[
|4 - 2| = 2
\]

Setting these distances equal gives:
\[
\sqrt{(h - 3)^2 + (k - 4)^2} = 2
\]
Squaring both sides, we get:
\[
(h - 3)^2 + (k - 4)^2 = 4
\]

The vertex of the parabola is midway between the focus and the directrix. For a standard parabola opening upwards or downwards, the vertex lies halfway between the focus and the directrix. The distance from the vertex to the directrix (or to the focus) is \( p \). 

Given that the directrix is \( y = 2 \), the vertex must be at a point where the \( y \)-coordinate is such that the distance from the vertex to the directrix is \( p \). The equation of the parabola can also be written in terms of its vertex.

However, since we are given an arbitrary point on the parabola and not necessarily requiring it to be the vertex, we solve for possible foci. 

We find that the focus must satisfy:
\[
(h - 3)^2 + (k - 4)^2 = 4
\]

Given the standard form of a parabola with directrix \( y = 2 \) and focus \( (h, k) \), we solve for \( h \) and \( k \). The vertex is at \( (h, k + p) \) or \( (h, 2 - p) \), but since the point \( (3, 4) \) must lie on the parabola, we find that there are two possible foci: one to the left of \( x = 3 \) and one to the right.

Thus, solving for \( h \) gives:
\[
h - 3 = \pm \frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3}
\]

This results in two distinct focuses at \( (3 + \frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3}, k) \) and \( (3 - \frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3}, k) \). Therefore, there are two parabolas satisfying the given conditions.

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{2}
\]

Comparing this with our given equation \( y^2 = 32x \), we can equate \( 4p = 32 \). Solving for \( p \), we get:

\[
p = \frac{32}{4} = 8
\]

Now that we have the value of \( p \):

1. **Finding the Focus:**
   The focus of a parabola that opens to the right is located at \( (p, 0) \). Substituting \( p = 8 \), the coordinates of the focus are:

   \[
   \text{Focus} = (8, 0)
   \]

2. **Finding the Directrix:**
   The directrix of such a parabola is a vertical line located at \( x = -p \). Substituting \( p = 8 \), the equation of the directrix is:

   \[
   \text{Directrix} = x = -8
   \]

In summary, for the given parabola \( y^2 = 32x \):

- **Focus:** (8, 0)
- **Directrix:** \( x = -8 \)

This completes our solution to finding both the focus and the directrix of the parabola.

Given that the directrix is \( x = -1 \) and the parabola passes through the point \( (2, 3) \), we need to find the equation of the parabola.

First, we recognize that the parabola can open in two different directions depending on the vertex's y-coordinate. We use the standard form of a horizontal parabola: \( (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \), where the directrix is given by \( x = h - p \).

1. The directrix is \( x = -1 \), so we have:
   \[
   h - p = -1
   \]
   Solving for \( h \):
   \[
   h = p - 1
   \]

2. The parabola passes through the point \( (2, 3) \). Substituting this into the standard form equation:
   \[
   (3 - k)^2 = 4p(2 - h)
   \]
   Since \( h = p - 1 \), substitute \( h \):
   \[
   (3 - k)^2 = 4p(2 - (p - 1)) = 4p(3 - p)
   \]

3. We know from the directrix that \( h = p - 1 \). Substituting this into the equation and solving for \( p \):
   \[
   (3 - k)^2 = 4p(3 - p)
   \]
   This leads to two possible values for \( p \) when considering the quadratic nature of the equation. Solving, we find that both cases yield a consistent result with \( p = \frac{3}{2} \).

4. Substituting \( p = \frac{3}{2} \) back into the equation for \( h \):
   \[
   h = \frac{3}{2} - 1 = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

5. Considering both possible vertices along the y-axis, we get two equations:
   - For vertex at \( (h, k) = (\frac{1}{2}, 0) \):
     \[
     (y - 0)^2 = 4 \cdot \frac{3}{2} \left( x - \frac{1}{2} \right)
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     y^2 = 6\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)
     \]
   - For vertex at \( (h, k) = (\frac{1}{2}, 6) \):
     \[
     (y - 6)^2 = 4 \cdot \frac{3}{2} \left( x - \frac{1}{2} \right)
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     (y - 6)^2 = 6\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)
     \]

Thus, the equations of the parabola are:

\[
\boxed{y^2 = 6\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)}
\]
and
\[
\boxed{(y - 6)^2 = 6\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)}
\]

To find the vertex of the parabola given by the equation \( Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we'll complete the square.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   Y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
   - Square this value to get \( 1 \).
   \[
   Y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1) + 5
   \]

4. **Adjust for the added value** by subtracting it outside the parentheses:
   \[
   Y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1) + 5 - 0 \quad (\text{since } 3(1) = 3, \text{ but we didn't add any constant term})
   \]

5. **Rewrite the perfect square trinomial as a squared binomial:**
   \[
   Y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 5
   \]

Now, the equation is in vertex form \( Y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), where:
- \( h = -1 \)
- \( k = 5 \)

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Vertex at } (-1, 5)}
\]

To convert the given quadratic equation into vertex form by completing the square for the \( x \) terms, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 1:** Isolate the \( x \)-terms and move other terms to one side.
\[
x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
\]

**Step 2:** Complete the square for the quadratic in \( x \).

- Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): 
  \[ \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \]
  
- Square this value to get the perfect square trinomial:
  \[
  (-2)^2 = 4
  \]

**Step 3:** Add and subtract this square inside the equation.
\[
x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4 = -8y - 12
\]
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 = -8y - 12
\]

**Step 4:** Simplify and solve for \( y \).
\[
(x - 2)^2 - 4 = -8y - 12
\]
\[
(x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8
\]
\[
(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
\]

The equation is now in the vertex form of a parabola:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)}
\]

Equation (3): 4a + 2b = 4
Equation (4): a - b = -1

I can solve this system of equations. Let's rearrange equation (4) to express a in terms of b.
From equation (4): a = b - 1.

Now, substitute a = b -1 into equation (3):
4*(b-1) + 2b = 4
Expanding: 4b - 4 + 2b = 4
Combine like terms: 6b - 4 = 4

To find the equation of the parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4), we use the general form y = ax² + bx + c.

Substituting each point into this equation:

1. For (2,8): 8 = 4a + 2b + c
2. For (-1,3): 3 = a - b + c
3. For (0,4): 4 = c

From the third equation, we find that c = 4.

Substituting c into the first two equations:

1. 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 4a + 2b = 4
2. 3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1

Solving these simultaneously, we find:
From equation 2: a = b - 1

Substituting into equation 1:
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 → 6b - 4 = 4 → 6b = 8 → b = 4/3

Then, a = (4/3) - 1 = 1/3.

The final equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

Looking at the problem again, the directrix is y = -4. The vertex is at (2, -1). So, to find p, I can use the formula that relates the directrix and the vertex. Since the directrix is y = k - p, plugging in the known values: -4 = (-1) - p. Let me solve for p.

-4 = -1 - p

-3 = -p

p = 3

(x - 2)^2 = 4 * 3 * (y + 1)  
Simplifying that:  
(x - 2)^2 = 12(y + 1)

To find the equation of the parabola with vertex (2, -1) and directrix y = -4, we use the standard form for a vertical parabola opening upwards: 

\[
(x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the vertex. Here, \(h = 2\) and \(k = -1\), so substituting these values gives:

\[
(x - 2)^2 = 4p(y + 1)
\]

The directrix of a vertical parabola is given by \(y = k - p\). Given the directrix y = -4, we set up the equation:

\[
-4 = -1 - p
\]

Solving for \(p\):

\[
-3 = -p \implies p = 3
\]

Substituting \(p = 3\) into the parabola's equation gives:

\[
(x - 2)^2 = 12(y + 1)
\]

**Answer:**

The equation of the parabola is \((x - 2)^2 = 12(y + 1)\).

To determine the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\), we use the standard form:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

**Given:**
- **Center:** \((5, -2)\)
- **Radius:** \(5\)

**Step 1:** Substitute the given values into the standard equation.

\[
x - 5 \quad \text{and} \quad y - (-2) = y + 2
\]

So, the equation becomes:

\[
(x - 5)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 5^2
\]

**Step 2:** Simplify \(5^2\) to get the final equation.

\[
(x - 5)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[

Finally, I'll substitute the center (2, -1) and radius √18 into the standard circle equation: (x - 2)² + (y + 1)² = (√18)², which simplifies to (x - 2)² + (y + 1)² = 18.

To find the equation of a circle when given two endpoints of its diameter, we can follow these steps:

1. **Find the Center (Midpoint):**
   
   The center of the circle is the midpoint of the diameter's endpoints \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\).
   
   \[
   h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
   \]
   
   \[
   k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
   \]
   
   **Center:** \((h, k) = (2, -1)\)

2. **Find the Radius:**
   
   The radius is half the distance between the two endpoints.
   
   Distance formula:
   
   \[
   r = \frac{\sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{64 + 16}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]
   
3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   
   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Substituting \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = 2\sqrt{5}\):
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
   \]
   
   Simplifying:
   
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{(x-2)^2+(y+1)^2=20}

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Group like terms:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. **Complete the square for \( x \):**
   - Coefficient of \( x \) is -4.
   - Take half of it: -4 / 2 = -2.
   - Square it: (-2)^2 = 4.
   - Add and subtract this inside the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4)
     \]
   - This becomes \( (x - 2)^2 - 4 \).

3. **Complete the square for \( y \):**
   - Coefficient of \( y \) is +2.
   - Take half of it: 2 / 2 = 1.
   - Square it: 1^2 = 1.
   - Add and subtract this inside the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 + 2y + 1 - 1)
     \]
   - This becomes \( (y + 1)^2 - 1 \).

4. **Rewrite the original equation with completed squares:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 - 4 + (y + 1)^2 - 1 = 11
   \]

5. **Combine constants on the right side:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

6. **Identify the center and radius:**
   - Center \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \)
   - Radius \( r = \sqrt{16} = 4 \)

**Final Answer:**  
The equation in standard form is:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]
with center at \( (2, -1) \) and radius \( 4 \).

To determine the equation of the circle with center \((-2, 5)\) that passes through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Write the General Equation of a Circle**

The standard form of a circle's equation is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

**Step 2: Plug in the Center Coordinates**

Given the center \((-2, 5)\), substitute \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\) into the equation:
\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]
Simplify to get:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]

**Step 3: Use the Given Point to Find \(r\)**
The circle passes through \((1, 9)\). Substitute \(x = 1\) and \(y = 9\) into the equation:
\[
(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2
\]
Calculate each part:
\[
3^2 = 9 \quad \text{and} \quad 4^2 = 16
\]
Add them together:
\[
9 + 16 = 25
\]
So, \(r^2 = 25\) and thus \(r = 5\).

**Step 4: Write the Complete Equation of the Circle**
Substitute \(r = 5\) back into the equation:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

The equation of an ellipse with foci at (±40, 0) and a major axis length of 100 can be determined using the standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin: 

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where:
- \( a \) is the semi-major axis,
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis,
- The distance between each focus and the center (\( c \)) is given by \( c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} \).

Given that the major axis length is 100, we find \( a = 50 \). Since the foci are at (±40, 0), \( c = 40 \). Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \):

\[
b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 50^2 - 40^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900
\]

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1
\]

To determine the area of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I'll start by identifying the standard form of an ellipse's equation, which is \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \). Here, (h,k) represents the center, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

In this case, comparing with the standard form:
- The equation is \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), so h=0 and k=0. Therefore, the center is at (0,0).
- Here, \( a^2 = 4 \) which means \( a = 2 \).
- Similarly, \( b^2 = 9 \) implies \( b = 3 \).

With a and b identified:
- The area of an ellipse is calculated using the formula \( A = \pi \times a \times b \).

Plugging in the values:
\( A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \)

Therefore, the area of the given ellipse is \( 6\pi \).
</think>

To determine the area of an ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Semi-Major and Semi-Minor Axes:**
   - The standard form of an ellipse's equation is:
     \[
     \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
     where (h, k) is the center, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.
   - Comparing with the given equation:
     - \( h = 0 \), \( k = 0 \)
     - \( a^2 = 4 \) ⇒ \( a = 2 \)
     - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

2. **Calculate the Area:**
   - The area of an ellipse is given by:
     \[
     A = \pi \times a \times b
     \]
   - Plugging in the values:
     \[
     A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
     \]

### Final Answer:

The area of the ellipse defined by \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \) is:

To determine the foci and vertices of the given ellipse \(\frac{(x - 1)^2}{9} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{16} = 1\), we proceed as follows:

1. **Identify the standard form**: The equation is in the form \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1\), which represents a vertical ellipse with \(a > b\).

2. **Determine the center, vertices, and foci**:
   - The center of the ellipse is at \((h, k) = (1, -2)\).
   - Here, \(a^2 = 16\) so \(a = 4\), giving the major axis length.
   - \(b^2 = 9\) so \(b = 3\), providing the minor axis length.

3. **Calculate vertices**:
   - Vertices along the y-axis are located at a distance of \(a\) from the center: \((1, -2 + 4) = (1, 2)\) and \((1, -2 - 4) = (1, -6)\).

   - Foci along the y-axis are at: \((1, -2 + \sqrt{7})\) and \((1, -2 - \sqrt{7})\).

Vertices: (1, 2) and (1, -6), Foci: (1, sqrt(7)-2) and (1, -sqrt(7)-2)

To solve for the asymptotes and foci of the hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \):

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   The given equation is a standard form of a horizontally opening hyperbola:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   Here, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), so the center is at (0, 0).

2. **Determine \( a \) and \( b \):**
   From the equation:
   \[
   a^2 = 9 \Rightarrow a = 3
   \]
   \[
   b^2 = 16 \Rightarrow b = 4
   \]

3. **Find the Asymptotes:**
   For a horizontally opening hyperbola, asymptotes are given by:
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x + k
   \]
   Substituting values:
   \[
   y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x
   \]

4. **Calculate the Foci:**
   The distance from the center to each focus is \( c \), where:
   \[
   c^2 = a^2 + b^2
   \]
   Substituting values:
   \[
   c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \Rightarrow c = 5
   \]
   Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, the foci are at \( (c, 0) \) and \( (-c, 0) \):
   \[
   Foci: (5, 0) \text{ and } (-5, 0)
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The asymptotes of the hyperbola are \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \) and \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \), and the foci are located at (5, 0) and (-5, 0).

Now compute each coefficient.

X² term:

(cos²θ)/9 - (sin²θ)/16 = (9/13)/9 - (4/13)/16

= 1/13 - 4/(13*16)

= 1/13 - 1/(52)

Convert to common denominator:

= 4/52 - 1/52 = 3/52.

Y² term:

(sin²θ)/9 - (cos²θ)/16 = (4/13)/9 - (9/13)/16

= 4/(117) - 9/(208)

Hmm, to combine these, let's find a common denominator. Let me compute each separately:

Wait, perhaps it's easier to keep as fractions:

But actually, maybe I made a mistake here because when you have the equation set up with X and Y, that would be in terms of the rotated coordinates. But since we are expressing everything around the center (-1, 1), does this affect how we handle the rotation?

Wait no, perhaps not, but let me think again.

Wait, maybe I should express x' = (x - h) cosθ - (y - k) sinθ

Similarly, y' = (x - h) sinθ + (y - k) cosθ

So actually, in the equation ((x')²)/9 - ((y')²)/16 = 1,

x' and y' are linear transformations of x and y centered at (-1,1).

Therefore, substituting:

[(3/sqrt(13))(x + 1) - (2/sqrt(13))(y - 1)]^2 /9 - [(2/sqrt(13))(x + 1) + (3/sqrt(13))(y - 1)]^2 /16 = 1

Let me simplify each bracket.

First, for x':

= [3(x+1)/sqrt(13) - 2(y-1)/sqrt(13)] 

= [3(x+1) - 2(y-1)]/sqrt(13)

Similarly, y':

= [2(x+1) + 3(y-1)]/sqrt(13)

So the equation becomes:

([3(x+1)-2(y-1)]²)/(9*13) - ([2(x+1)+3(y-1)]²)/(16*13) = 1

Multiply both sides by 13 to eliminate denominators:

[ (3x +3 -2y +2)^2 ] /9 - [ (2x +2 +3y -3)^2 ]/16 = 13

Simplify inside the brackets.

First term numerator: 3x -2y +5, squared over 9.

Second term numerator: 2x +3y -1, squared over 16.

So equation becomes:

( (3x - 2y +5)^2 ) /9 - ( (2x + 3y -1)^2 )/16 = 13

Now expand both squares in the numerators.

First term: (3x -2y +5)^2 

= (3x)^2 + (-2y)^2 + (5)^2 + 2*(3x)(-2y) + 2*(3x)*5 + 2*(-2y)*5

=9x² +4y² +25 -12xy +30x -20y.

Similarly, second term: (2x +3y -1)^2 

= (2x)^2 + (3y)^2 + (-1)^2 + 2*(2x)(3y) + 2*(2x)*(-1) + 2*(3y)*(-1)

=4x² +9y² +1 +12xy -4x -6y.

Now substitute back into the equation:

[ (9x² +4y² +25 -12xy +30x -20y) ] /9 - [ (4x² +9y² +1 +12xy -4x -6y) ]/16 = 13

Now, split each fraction:

First fraction: 

= 9x²/9 + 4y²/9 +25/9 -12xy/9 +30x/9 -20y/9.

Simplify each term:

= x² + (4y²)/9 +25/9 - (4xy)/3 + (10x)/3 - (20y)/9

Second fraction:

[4x² +9y² +1 +12xy -4x -6y]/16 

Split into terms:

=4x²/16 +9y²/16 +1/16 +12xy/16 -4x/16 -6y/16.

Simplify each term:

=(x²)/4 + (9y²)/16 + 1/16 + (3xy)/4 - x/4 - (3y)/8

Now, subtract the second fraction from the first and set equal to 13.

So,

[ x² + (4y²)/9 +25/9 - (4xy)/3 + (10x)/3 - (20y)/9 ] 

- [ (x²)/4 + (9y²)/16 + 1/16 + (3xy)/4 - x/4 - (3y)/8 ] =13

Now, distribute the negative sign into each term of the second fraction:

= x² + (4y²)/9 +25/9 - (4xy)/3 + (10x)/3 - (20y)/9 

- x²/4 - 9y²/16 - 1/16 -3xy/4 +x/4 +3y/8 =13

Now, combine like terms.

First, for x²:

= x² - x²/4 = (4x² -x²)/4 = (3x²)/4

For y²:

= (4y²)/9 - 9y²/16 

To subtract these fractions, find a common denominator of 144.

(4y² *16)/(144) - (9y²*9)/(144)

=64y²/144 -81y²/144 = (-17y²)/144

For xy:

= -4xy/3 -3xy/4 

Convert to common denominator 12.

-16xy/12 -9xy/12 = -25xy/12

For x terms:

= (10x)/3 +x/4 

Common denominator 12: 40x/12 +3x/12 =43x/12

For y terms:

=-20y/9 +3y/8 

Common denominator72.

-20y*8= -160y; 3y*9=27y. So total (-160+27)y /72 = (-133y)/72

Constants:

25/9 -1/16 

Convert to common denominator 144.

25*16/(144) -1*9/(144)= 400/144 -9/144=391/144

So putting all together:

(3x²)/4 + (-17y²)/144 -25xy/12 +43x/12 -133y/72 +391/144 =13

Now, multiply both sides by 144 to eliminate denominators.

Compute each term:

(3x²)/4 *144= (3*144)x² /4= 3*36 x²=108x²

(-17y²)/144 *144= -17y²

-25xy/12 *144= -25xy*(12)= -300xy

43x/12 *144= 43x*12= 516x

(-133y)/72 *144=-133y*2= -266y

391/144 *144=391

Right side:13*144=1872

So, equation becomes:

108x² -17y² -300xy +516x -266y +391 = 1872

Bring all terms to left-hand side (subtract 1872):

108x² -17y² -300xy +516x -266y +391 -1872=0

Simplify constants:

391 -1872 =-1481

So,

108x² -17y² -300xy +516x -266y -1481=0

This is the expanded form of the hyperbola.

Now, perhaps factor coefficients or see if any simplification can be done. However, this equation is already in general quadratic form: Ax² + Bxy + Cy² + Dx + Ey + F = 0.

Looking at the problem again, it just says "what is S(S)?$"

Wait, initial prompt was:

"SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS/SYS Next, recall that for a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin, the standard form is \(\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(c\) represents the distance from the center to each focus and satisfies the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\).

Given that one of the vertices is at (0, ±3), this means \(a = 3\). Squaring both sides gives \(a^2 = 9\).

To find the equation of the hyperbola with foci at (0, ±5) and a vertex at (0, ±3), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify the Type of Hyperbola**

Since the foci are on the y-axis, it's a **vertical hyperbola**. The standard form for such a hyperbola centered at the origin is:
\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \(a\) is the distance from the center to each vertex, and \(c\) is the distance from the center to each focus.

**Step 2: Determine \(a\) and \(c\)**

Given:
- One vertex at (0, ±3) implies \(a = 3\).
- Foci at (0, ±5) imply \(c = 5\).

**Step 3: Use the Relationship Between \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\)**

For hyperbolas, the relationship is given by:
\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Plugging in the known values:
\[
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \\
25 = 9 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 16
\]

**Step 4: Write the Equation of the Hyperbola**

Substitute \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) into the standard form:
\[
\frac{y^2}{3^2} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \\
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1}

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

\[
y = k \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)
\]

Since our center (h, k) is at (0, 0), this simplifies to:

\[
y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x

\frac{b}{a} = \frac{3}{5}

\frac{b}{5} = \frac{3}{5}

\frac{x^2}{25} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1

The problem involves finding the equation of a vertical hyperbola given its vertices at (0,5) and (0,-3). 

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Determine the center**: The center of the hyperbola is the midpoint between the two vertices. So,
   - Center coordinates = ((0 + 0)/2, (5 + (-3))/2) = (0, 1).

2. **Calculate 'a'**: The distance from the center to each vertex gives us 'a'.
   - a = |5 - 1| = 4.

3. **Standard form of the equation**: For a vertical hyperbola centered at (h, k), the standard equation is:
   \[
   \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   Plugging in known values:
   \[
   \frac{(y - 1)^2}{4^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
   \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

Since the value of 'b' isn't provided, we can only express the equation in terms of 'b'. If additional information is given (like foci locations or asymptotes), 'b' could be calculated. 

Final Answer:  
\[
\boxed{\frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1}
\]

First, I recognize that the problem involves finding the equation of a parabola given its vertex and focus. The vertex is at (0, 0), and the focus is at (0, 4). 

Since both points lie on the y-axis, this indicates that the parabola opens upwards.

The standard form of such a parabola's equation is \( y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \), where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), we have p = 4. Substituting this into the formula:

\( y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

Thus, the equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).
</think>

To determine the equation of a parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4), we follow these steps:

1. **Identify the type of parabola**: Since both the vertex and focus lie on the y-axis, the parabola opens upwards.

2. **Use the standard form of a parabola's equation**:
   
   The standard form for a parabola that opens upwards with vertex at (0, 0) is:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2
   \]
   
   where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

3. **Determine the value of \( p \)**:
   
   Given that the focus is at (0, 4), we have:
   
   \[
   p = 4
   \]

4. **Substitute \( p \) into the equation**:
   
   Plugging \( p = 4 \) into the standard form:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{4 \times 4}x^2 = \frac{1}{16}x^2
   \]

5. **Final Equation of the Parabola**:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{16}x^2
   \]

To solve for the focus and directrix of the parabola given by the equation \( y^2 = 32x \), I start by recognizing that this is a standard form of a horizontally opening parabola.

The general form for such a parabola is \( y^2 = 4px \), where \( p \) represents the distance from the vertex to both the focus and the directrix. 

By comparing \( y^2 = 32x \) with the standard equation, I determine that \( 4p = 32 \). Solving for \( p \), I divide both sides by 4, yielding \( p = 8 \).

With \( p = 8 \), the focus of the parabola is located at \( (p, 0) \), which means it is at \( (8, 0) \).

To determine the focus and directrix of the parabola given by the equation \( y^2 = 32x \):

1. **Identify the Standard Form**:
   The given equation is in the standard form of a horizontally opening parabola:  
   \[
   y^2 = 4px
   \]
   
2. **Compare with the Given Equation**:
   Compare \( y^2 = 32x \) with \( y^2 = 4px \):
   \[
   4p = 32
   \]

3. **Solve for \( p \)**:
   \[
   p = \frac{32}{4} = 8
   \]

4. **Determine the Focus**:
   The focus of a horizontally opening parabola is located at \( (p, 0) \):
   \[
   \text{Focus} = (8, 0)
   \]
   
5. **Determine the Directrix**:
   The directrix is the vertical line \( x = -p \):
   \[
   \text{Directrix} = x = -8
   \]

6. **Final Answer**:
   The focus of the parabola is at \( (8, 0) \), and its directrix is the line \( x = -8 \).

\[
\boxed{\text{Focus: }(8,\ 0),\ \text{Directrix: }x = -8}
\]

Given the problem of finding the equation of a parabola with directrix \( x = -1 \) and passing through the point \( (2, 3) \), we start by noting that the standard form of a parabola opening to the right is \( (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \), where the vertex is at \( (h, k) \) and the directrix is \( x = h - p \).

1. The directrix given is \( x = -1 \), so we have:
   \[
   h - p = -1 \implies h = -1 + p
   \]

2. We assume the vertex \( (h, k) \) for simplicity. For this problem, we can set \( k = 0 \) to simplify calculations.

3. Substituting the point \( (2, 3) \) into the equation:
   \[
   (3 - 0)^2 = 4p(2 - (-1 + p))
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   9 = 4p(3 - p)
   \]

4. Solving the quadratic equation \( 4p^2 - 12p + 9 = 0 \):
   \[
   (2p - 3)^2 = 0 \implies p = \frac{3}{2}
   \]

5. Substituting \( p = \frac{3}{2} \) back into the standard form equation:
   \[
   y^2 = 4 \left( \frac{3}{2} \right) (x - (-1 + \frac{3}{2}))
   \]
   Simplifying further, we get:
   \[
   y^2 = 6(x - 0.5)
   \]

6. Therefore, the equation of the parabola is:
   \[
   y^2 = 6x - 3
   \]

\[
\boxed{y^2 = 6x - 3}

To find the vertex of the parabola defined by the equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **Isolate the Quadratic Terms**:
   Start by moving all constant terms to one side to prepare for completing the square.

   \]

   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) + 8y = -12 + 4
   \]

   The left side becomes a perfect square, and simplify the right side.

   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + 8y = -8

   \[

   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
   \]

   \[

   \]

Sure, let's solve the problem step-by-step.

**Given:**
The equation of the parabola is:
\[ y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \]

**Objective:**
Determine the axis of symmetry, focus, and directrix of the parabola.

---

### Step 1: Identify the Vertex
The given equation is in the vertex form of a parabola:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
where \((h, k)\) is the vertex.

Comparing with the given equation:
- \( h = 1 \)
- \( k = 5 \)

**Vertex:** \((1, 5)\)

---

### Step 2: Determine the Axis of Symmetry
The axis of symmetry for a parabola in the form \( y = (x - h)^2 + k \) is the vertical line passing through the vertex. Therefore, it is:
\[ x = h \]

**Axis of Symmetry:** \( x = 1 \)

---

### Step 3: Find the Focus
For a parabola of the form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), where \(a\) determines how wide or narrow the parabola is:
- If \( a > 0 \), it opens upward.
- The focus is located at:
\[ (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \]

From our equation:
- Comparing with standard form, \( a = 1 \)
  
Plugging in the values:
- Focus: \( \left(1, 5 + \frac{1}{4 \times 1}\right) = (1, 5.25) \)

---

### Step 4: Find the Directrix
The directrix is a horizontal line that is equidistant from the vertex but in the opposite direction of the focus:
\[ y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \]

Plugging in the values:
- Directrix: \( y = 5 - \frac{1}{4 \times 1} = 4.75 \)

---

### Final Answer
**Focus:** \( (1, 5.25) \)  
**Directrix:** \( y = 4.75 \)

    'Focus': { 'x': 1, 'y': 5.25 },
    'Directrix': 'y = 4.75'
}

Then there's (-1, 3). Plugging x=-1 and y=3 into the equation gives me 3 = a*(-1)² + b*(-1) + 4. Simplifying that, it becomes 3 = a - b + 4. Subtracting 4 from both sides, I get -1 = a - b.

1) 4a + 2b = 4
2) a - b = -1

I need to solve these simultaneously to find the values of a and b. Let me see how best to approach this system of equations. Maybe substitution would work here since equation 2 is simpler.

4*(b - 1) + 2b = 4

Lastly, (-1,3): y = (1/3)(1) + (4/3)(-1) + 4 = 1/3 - 4/3 + 4 = (-3/3) + 4 = -1 + 4 = 3. Perfect.

\[
(h, k) = \left(\frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}\right)
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is at (2, -1).

\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Therefore, the radius \( r \) is half of this distance:
\[
r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}

   \[

   \]

   \]

   \]

To convert this into the standard form \( (X - h)^2 + (Y - k)^2 = r^2 \), I will complete the square for both the \( X \) and \( Y \) terms.

**Completing the Square for \( X \):**

1. Start with the \( X \) terms: \( X^2 + 6X \).
2. Take half of the coefficient of \( X \), which is \( \frac{6}{2} = 3 \), and square it to get \( 9 \).
3. Add and subtract this square inside the equation:
   \( (X^2 + 6X + 9) - 9 \)
4. This can be written as: \( (X + 3)^2 - 9 \).

**Completing the Square for \( Y \):**

1. Start with the \( Y \) terms: \( Y^2 - 4Y \).
2. Take half of the coefficient of \( Y \), which is \( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \), and square it to get \( 4 \).
3. Add and subtract this square inside the equation:
   \( (Y^2 - 4Y + 4) - 4 \)
4. This can be written as: \( (Y - 2)^2 - 4 \).

**Combining Everything:**

Substitute the completed squares back into the original equation:

\( (X + 3)^2 - 9 + (Y - 2)^2 - 4 - 12 = 0 \)

Combine the constants on the left side:

\( (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 - 25 = 0 \)

Move the constant term to the right side of the equation:

\( (X + 3)^2 + (Y - 2)^2 = 25 \)

**Interpreting the Standard Form:**

The standard form \( (X - h)^2 + (Y - k)^2 = r^2 \) reveals that:
- The center of the circle is at \( (-h, -k) \), which in this case is \( (-3, 2) \).
- The radius \( r \) is the square root of 25, so \( r = 5 \).

This completes the conversion to the standard form and identifies the circle's center and radius.

   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

x² + (x² + 6x + 9) = 17

Combine like terms:

2x² + 6x + 9 = 17

Subtract 17 from both sides to set the equation to zero:

2x² + 6x - 8 = 0

Hmm, that's a quadratic equation. Let me see if I can simplify it first.

Divide all terms by 2 to make it easier:

x² + 3x - 4 = 0

Now, factoring this should be straightforward since the coefficients are small. Looking for two numbers that multiply to -4 and add up to 3. That would be 4 and -1.

So, (x + 4)(x - 1) = 0

Setting each factor equal to zero gives x = -4 or x = 1.

Now, plug these x-values back into the line equation y = x + 3 to find their corresponding y-coordinates.

For x = -4:

y = (-4) + 3 = -1

So one point is (-4, -1).

For x = 1:

y = 1 + 3 = 4

The other point is (1, 4).

Let me double-check these points to make sure they satisfy both equations.

First, checking (-4, -1):

Circle: (-4)² + (-1)² = 16 + 1 = 17 ✔️
Line: y = x + 3 → -1 = -4 + 3 → -1 = -1 ✔️

Now for (1, 4):

Circle: 1² + 4² = 1 + 16 = 17 ✔️
Line: y = 1 + 3 = 4 ✔️

Looks good. So both points are correct.

Therefore, the intersection points are (-4, -1) and (1, 4).
</think>

The line \( y = x + 3 \) intersects the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) at two points: 

- First point: (-4, -1)
- Second point: (1, 4)

These are the exact coordinates where both geometric shapes meet.

To find the equation of a circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1):

1. **General Equation**: The general form of a circle is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

2. **Setting Up Equations**:
   - For (1, 1): \((1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2\)
   - For (2, -3): \((2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2\)
   - For (5, -1): \((5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2\)

3. **Subtracting Equations**:
   - Subtract the first equation from the second: \(4h + 8k = -10\) → Simplifies to \(2h + 4k = -5\)
   - Subtract the first equation from the third: \(8h + 4k = 24\) → Simplifies to \(2h + k = 6\)

4. **Solving for \(h\) and \(k\)**:
   - From step 3, we have two equations:
     - \(2h + 4k = -5\)
     - \(2h + k = 6\)
   - Subtract the second equation from the first: \(3k = -11\) → \(k = -\frac{11}{3}\)

5. **Finding \(h\)**:
   - Substitute \(k\) into one of the equations, say \(2h + (-\frac{11}{3}) = 6\) → \(2h = \frac{29}{3}\) → \(h = \frac{29}{6}\)

6. **Calculating Radius**:
   - Substitute \(h\) and \(k\) into the first equation to find \(r\): 
     - \( (1 - \frac{29}{6})^2 + (1 + \frac{11}{3})^2 = r^2 \)
     - Simplify: \((-\\frac{23}{6})^2 + (\frac{14}{3})^2 = r^2\)
     - Calculate: \(r^2 = \\frac{529}{36} + \\frac{196}{9} = \\frac{529 + 784}{36} = \\frac{1313}{36}\)
     - Thus, \(r = \sqrt{\\frac{1313}{36}} = \\frac{\sqrt{1313}}{6}\)

**Final Answer**: The equation of the circle is:

\[
(x - \frac{29}{6})^2 + (y + \frac{11}{3})^2 = \left(\frac{\sqrt{1313}}{6}\right)^2
\]

Alright, let's solve this problem step by step to find the radius and the center of the circle given by the equation:

\[
X^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
\]

### Step 1: Rearrange the Equation

First, let's rewrite the equation grouping \(x\) terms together and \(y\) terms together.

\[
X^2 - 8x + y^2 - 6y = -20
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \(x\)

Take the coefficients of \(x\) (which is \(-8\)), divide by 2, square it:

\[
\left(\frac{-8}{2}\right)^2 = (-4)^2 = 16
\]

Add this to both sides.

\[
X^2 - 8x + 16 + y^2 - 6y = -20 + 16 \\
(X - 4)^2 + y^2 - 6y = -4
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \(y\)

Take the coefficients of \(y\) (which is \(-6\)), divide by 2, square it:

\[
\left(\frac{-6}{2}\right)^2 = (-3)^2 = 9
\]

Add this to both sides.

\[
(X - 4)^2 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = -4 + 9 \\
(X - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
\]

### Step 4: Interpret the Standard Form

The equation is now in standard form:

\[
(X - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

Comparing, we have:
- Center: \((4, 3)\)
- Radius: \(\sqrt{5}\)

### Final Answer

Next, I use the standard form of a circle's equation:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) are the coordinates of the center. Substituting the given center into the equation gives:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Plugging in the coordinates:
\[
r = \sqrt{(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

Now that I have the radius, I substitute \(r^2\) back into the equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25

To find the equation of the circle with center \((1, 2)\) and passing through the point \((4, 6)\), we use the standard form of a circle's equation:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle, and \(r\) is its radius.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Center and Point:**
   - Center (\(h, k\)) = (1, 2)
   - Point on the Circle = (4, 6)

2. **Calculate the Radius (\(r\)):**
   
   The radius is the distance between the center \((1, 2)\) and the point \((4, 6)\). We use the distance formula:
   
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the values:
   
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the circle is:

\[

To determine the eccentricity of the given ellipse, I first identify the standard form of an ellipse equation.

The general equation is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a\) is the semi-major axis and \(b\) is the semi-minor axis.

In this case, comparing with the given equation \(\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\), I find that \(a^2 = 36\) and \(b^2 = 9\).

Calculating \(a\) and \(b\):
\(a = \sqrt{36} = 6\)
\(b = \sqrt{9} = 3\)

Next, I use the formula for eccentricity of an ellipse:
\(e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a}\)

Substituting the values:
\(e = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6}\)

Simplifying \(\sqrt{27}\) gives \(3\sqrt{3}\), so:
\(e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\)
</think>

To determine the **eccentricity** of the given ellipse, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the standard form of the ellipse equation:**
   
   The general equation of an ellipse is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where \( a \) is the semi-major axis and \( b \) is the semi-minor axis.

2. **Compare with the given equation:**
   
   Given:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]
   
   Therefore:
   \[
   a^2 = 36 \quad \text{and} \quad b^2 = 9
   \]

3. **Calculate \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   \[
   a = \sqrt{a^2} = \sqrt{36} = 6
   \]
   \[
   b = \sqrt{b^2} = \sqrt{9} = 3
   \]

4. **Use the formula for eccentricity (\( e \)) of an ellipse:**
   
   The eccentricity is given by:
   \[
   e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a}
   \]
   
5. **Substitute the values into the formula:**
   
   \[
   e = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6}
   \]
   
6. **Simplify \( \sqrt{27} \):**
   
   \[
   \sqrt{27} = \sqrt{9 \times 3} = 3\sqrt{3}
   \]
   
7. **Final calculation:**
   
   \[
   e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\[
\boxed{\text{'eccentricity' : }\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}}
\]

Now, plugging a² and b² into the standard form of the ellipse equation: \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), which becomes \(\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1\).

The given equation represents an ellipse with semi-major axis \( b = 3 \) and semi-minor axis \( a = 2 \). The area of an ellipse is calculated as \( \pi \times a \times b \).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

   - \( a^2 = 4 \) ⇒ \( a = 2 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

   - \( \text{Area} = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \)

To find the equation of an ellipse with a major axis length of 12 and a minor axis length of 8, centered at (-2, 1), we follow these steps:

1. **Determine Semi-Major Axis (a):**
   - Major axis length = 2 * semi-major axis
   - \( 2a = 12 \)  
   - \( a = 6 \)

2. **Determine Semi-Minor Axis (b):**
   - Minor axis length = 2 * semi-minor axis  
   - \( 2b = 8 \)  
   - \( b = 4 \)

3. **Standard Equation of an Ellipse:**
   - The standard form is:
     \[
     \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   - Where \( (h, k) \) is the center of the ellipse.

4. **Substitute Values:**
   - Center \( (-2, 1) \), so \( h = -2 \), \( k = 1 \)
   - Substitute into standard equation:
     \[
     \frac{(x + 2)^2}{6^2} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{4^2} = 1
     \]

5. **Simplify Denominators:**
   - \( 6^2 = 36 \)  
   - \( 4^2 = 16 \)

Final Equation:
\[
\frac{(x + 2)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - 1)^2}{16} = 1

The problem involves writing the equation of an ellipse given its center and the lengths of its major and minor axes. The standard form of an ellipse's equation is used for this purpose.

**Solution:**

1. **Given Information:**
   - Center (\(h, k\)) = (4, -3)
   - Length of Major Axis = 14
   - Length of Minor Axis = 10

2. **Calculate Semi-Axis Lengths:**
   - Semi-Major Axis (\(a\)) = \( \frac{14}{2} = 7 \)
   - Semi-Minor Axis (\(b\)) = \( \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)

3. **Ellipse Equation:**
   
   The standard form of an ellipse with center at (h, k) is:
   \[
   \frac{(x-h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{a^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   \frac{(x-4)^2}{5^2} + \frac{(y+3)^2}{7^2} = 1
   \]

   \[
   \frac{(x-4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y+3)^2}{49} = 1
   \]

To find the equation of a parabola with its vertex at \((0, 0)\) and focus at \((0, 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Direction of the Parabola
Since the vertex is at \((0, 0)\) and the focus is at \((0, 4)\), the parabola opens **upward**.

### Step 2: Use the Standard Form of a Vertical Parabola
The standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex at \((h, k)\) is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]

Given the vertex \((0, 0)\), the equation simplifies to:
\[ y = ax^2 \]

### Step 3: Find the Value of \(a\)
The relationship between \(a\) and the focal length \(p\) for a vertical parabola is given by:
\[ p = \frac{1}{4a} \]

Given that the focus is at \((0, 4)\), we know:
\[ p = 4 \]

Plugging this into the equation:
\[ 4 = \frac{1}{4a} \]
\[ 16a = 1 \]
\[ a = \frac{1}{16} \]

### Step 4: Write the Final Equation
Substitute \(a\) back into the standard form:
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: \left\{ \text{Final Answer} : \dfrac{x^2}{16} \right\}}
\]

Given the problem of finding the value of \( p \) for a parabola defined by \( y^2 = 4p(x - h) \) and knowing that it passes through the point \((-1, -k)\), we start by substituting this point into the equation.

Substituting \( x = -1 \) and \( y = -k \) into the parabola's equation:

\[
(-k)^2 = 4p(-1 - h)
\]

Simplifying this, we get:

\[
k^2 = 4p(-1 - h)
\]

Rearranging to solve for \( p \):

\[
k^2 = -4p(1 + h)
\]

\[
p = -\dfrac{k^2}{4(1 + h)}
\]

Thus, the value of \( p \) is:

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{k^2}{4(1 + h)}}
\]

Given the vertex at (5, -2), I substitute h = 5 and k = -2 into the standard form equation to get \( y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2 \).

To find the value of 'a', I use the distance between the vertex and the focus. The distance is 2 units from the vertex (y-coordinate difference) which equals \( \frac{1}{4|a|} \). Solving for 'a' gives me a = -1/8 since the parabola opens downward.

Finally, substituting 'a' back into the equation, the final equation of the parabola is \( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2 \).

I need to find the vertex form of the parabola given by Y = 3x² + 6x + 5.

First, I'll write down the standard form of a quadratic equation and identify the coefficients:
Y = ax² + bx + c

In this case, a = 3, b = 6, and c = 5.

Next, I'll use the vertex formula to find the x-coordinate of the vertex:
x = -b / (2a)
Plugging in the values:
x = -6 / (2*3) = -1

Now, I'll substitute this x-value back into the original equation to find the corresponding y-coordinate:
Y = 3(-1)² + 6*(-1) + 5
Simplifying:
Y = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2

Finally, I'll write the vertex form of the equation using the vertex coordinates (-1, 2):
Y = 3(x + 1)² + 2

   \[
   x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
   \]

2. **Complete the Square for \( x \)**: Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4 \), divide by 2 to get \( -2 \), and then square it to obtain \( 4 \). Add this to both sides.
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) = -8y - 12 + 4
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   (x-2)^2 = -8y - 8
   \]

3. **Factor Out the Coefficient of \( y \)**: Factor out \( -8 \) from the right side.
   \[
   (x-2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
   \]

4. **Compare to Vertex Form**: The equation now matches the standard vertex form \( (x-h)^2 = 4p(y-k) \), where the vertex is at \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \).

We need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three given points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). A general quadratic equation has the form:

y = ax² + bx + c

Using each point, we can set up equations to solve for the coefficients a, b, and c.

For the point (0, 4):

4 = a(0)² + b(0) + c → c = 4

Now, substitute c into the equation:

y = ax² + bx + 4

Using the point (2, 8):

8 = a(2)² + b(2) + 4
8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 4a + 2b = 4 → 2a + b = 2 ... (1)

Using the point (-1, 3):

3 = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4
3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1 ... (2)

Now, solve equations (1) and (2):

From equation (1): 2a + b = 2

From equation (2): a - b = -1

Adding both equations:

3a = 1 → a = 1/3

Substitute a into equation (2):

(1/3) - b = -1 → -b = -4/3 → b = 4/3

Now, the quadratic equation is:

y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4
</think>

To find the equation of the parabola that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4), we start with the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

Using each point to set up equations for \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \):

1. For the point (0, 4):
   \[
   4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \implies c = 4
   \]
   
2. For the point (2, 8):
   \[
   8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
   8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \\
   4a + 2b = 4 \implies 2a + b = 2 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
   \]

3. For the point (-1, 3):
   \[
   3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
   3 = a - b + 4 \\
   a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
   \]

Now, solve Equations 1 and 2:

\[
\begin{cases}
2a + b = 2 \\
a - b = -1
\end{cases}
\]

Adding both equations to eliminate \( b \):

\[
3a = 1 \implies a = \frac{1}{3}
\]

Substitute \( a = \frac{1}{3} \) into Equation 2:

\[
\frac{1}{3} - b = -1 \\
-b = -\frac{4}{3} \implies b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, the quadratic equation is:

\[ y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the parabola passing through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4) is:
\[
y = \boxed{\frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4}
\]

To convert the equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) into the standard form of a circle's equation, follow these steps:

**Step 1: Group like terms**

Start by grouping the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:
\[
x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y = 11
\]

**Step 2: Complete the square for \( x \) and \( y \)**

- For the \( x \)-terms (\( x^2 - 4x \)):
  - Coefficient of \( x \) is -4.
  - Divide by 2: -2.
  - Square it: (-2)^2 = 4.

- For the \( y \)-terms (\( y^2 + 2y \)):
  - Coefficient of \( y \) is +2.
  - Divide by 2: +1.
  - Square it: (1)^2 = 1.

**Step 3: Add these squares to both sides**

Add the squares obtained for completing the square on both sides:
\[
x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 + 2y + 1 = 11 + 4 + 1
\]

Simplify:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

**Final Answer**
The equation in the standard form is \(\boxed{(x-2)^{2}+(y+1)^{2}=16}\), with center at \( (2, -1) \) and radius \(4\).

Starting with the original equation:
\[ X^2 + Y^2 - 8X - 6Y + 20 = 0 \]

**Completing the Square for \( X \):**
The coefficient of \( X \) is -8. Half of -8 is -4, and squaring it gives 16.

**Completing the Square for \( Y \):**
The coefficient of \( Y \) is -6. Half of -6 is -3, and squaring it gives 9.

Adding these squares to both sides:
\[ X^2 - 8X + 16 + Y^2 - 6Y + 9 = -20 + 16 + 9 \]
\[ (X - 4)^2 + (Y - 3)^2 = 5 \]

Now, the equation is in standard form:
\[ (X - 4)^2 + (Y - 3)^2 = (\sqrt{5})^2 \]

To determine the **center** and **radius** of the circle given by the equation:

\[
X^2 + Y^2 - 8X - 6Y + 20 = 0
\]

we will rearrange it to match the standard form of a circle's equation, which is:

\[
(X - h)^2 + (Y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 1: Group Terms

Group the \(X\) terms and \(Y\) terms together:

\[
(X^2 - 8X) + (Y^2 - 6Y) = -20
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \(X\)

Take half of the coefficient of \(X\) (-8), which is \(-4\), and square it to get \(16\). Add this inside the equation:

\[
(X^2 - 8X + 16) = (X - 4)^2
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \(Y\)

Take half of the coefficient of \(Y\) (-6), which is \(-3\), and square it to get \(9\). Add this inside the equation:

\[
(Y^2 - 6Y + 9) = (Y - 3)^2
\]

### Step 4: Adjust the Equation

Add these squares to both sides of the equation:

\[
(X - 4)^2 + (Y - 3)^2 = -20 + 16 + 9
\]
\[
(X - 4)^2 + (Y - 3)^2 = 5
\]

### Step 5: Identify Center and Radius

The equation is now in standard form:

\[
(X - h)^2 + (Y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Comparing, we have:
- \(h = 4\)
- \(k = 3\)
- \(r^2 = 5\) → \(r = \sqrt{5}\)

### Final Answer

The center of the circle is at \((4, 3)\) and its radius is \(\sqrt{5}\).

- **Length of the major axis:** 12 (vertical direction)
- **Length of the minor axis:** 8 (horizontal direction)

**Step 1: Determine the semi-major and semi-minor axes**

The semi-major axis (\(a\)) is half of the major axis length:
\[ a = \frac{12}{2} = 6 \]

The semi-minor axis (\(b\)) is half of the minor axis length:
\[ b = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \]

**Step 2: Write the standard equation of the ellipse**

Since the major axis is vertical, the standard form of the ellipse's equation centered at \( (h, k) \) is:
\[

\]

First, I'll start by understanding what is meant by "standard form" for an equation of an ellipse.

An ellipse in standard form has the equation:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively. If \( a > b \), it's a horizontal ellipse; if \( b > a \), it's a vertical ellipse.

The given problem involves finding the equation of an ellipse with specified vertices and foci.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the type of ellipse:**
   - The vertices are at (0,7) and (0,-7), which lie on the y-axis.
   - This indicates that the major axis is vertical.

2. **Determine 'a' (semi-major axis):**
   - The distance from the center to each vertex is 7 units.
   - Therefore, \( a = 7 \).
   - So, \( a^2 = 49 \).

3. **Find 'c' (distance from center to foci):**
   - Foci are at (0,5) and (0,-5), so the distance from the center is 5 units.
   - Thus, \( c = 5 \).
   - Therefore, \( c^2 = 25 \).

4. **Calculate 'b' (semi-minor axis):**
   - For an ellipse with a vertical major axis, the relationship between a, b, and c is:
     \[
     c^2 = a^2 - b^2
     \]
   - Plugging in known values:
     \[
     25 = 49 - b^2
     \]
   - Solving for \( b^2 \):
     \[
     b^2 = 49 - 25 = 24
     \]

5. **Write the standard equation of the ellipse:**
   - The standard form when major axis is vertical:
     \[
     \frac{y^2}{a^2} + \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   - Substituting \( a^2 = 49 \) and \( b^2 = 24 \):
     \[
     \frac{y^2}{49} + \frac{x^2}{24} = 1
     \]

I need to find the area of an ellipse given by the equation x²/4 + y²/9 = 1.

First, I'll identify the standard form of an ellipse's equation, which is x²/a² + y²/b² = 1. Comparing this with the given equation, I can see that a² = 4 and b² = 9.

Next, I'll solve for a and b by taking the square roots of a² and b². So, a = √4 = 2 and b = √9 = 3.

The area A of an ellipse is given by the formula A = π * a * b. Substituting the values of a and b into this formula, I get A = π * 2 * 3 = 6π.
</think>

To find the area of the ellipse defined by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the standard form of an ellipse:**
   
   The general equation of an ellipse centered at the origin is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Here, \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

2. **Compare with the given equation:**
   
   Given:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]
   
   This matches the standard form where:
   
   \[
   a^2 = 4 \quad \text{and} \quad b^2 = 9
   \]

3. **Find \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   Taking square roots:
   
   \[
   a = \sqrt{4} = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad b = \sqrt{9} = 3
   \]

4. **Calculate the area of the ellipse:**
   
   The area \( A \) of an ellipse is given by:
   
   \[
   A = \pi a b
   \]
   
   Substituting the values of \( a \) and \( b \):
   
   \[
   A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The area of the ellipse is:

\[
\boxed{6\pi}
\]

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We're given the equation of a hyperbola:

\[ \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \]

Our goal is to find its asymptotes and foci.

**Step 1: Identify the type of conic section**

The equation has an \( x^2 \) term positive and a \( y^2 \) term negative, so it's a hyperbola centered at the origin (0,0), opening horizontally.

**Step 2: Determine the standard form**

For a hyperbola opening horizontally, the standard form is:

\[ \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Comparing this with our equation:
- \( h = 0 \)
- \( k = 0 \)
- \( a^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( a = 3 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

**Step 3: Find the asymptotes**

The equations for the asymptotes of a horizontally opening hyperbola are:

\[ y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h) \]

Plugging in our values:
- \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \)

So, the asymptotes are:
- **First Asymptote:** \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \)
- **Second Asymptote:** \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \)

**Step 4: Calculate the foci**

The distance from the center to each focus (\( c \)) is given by:

\[ c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} \]

Substituting our values:
- \( c = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5 \)

Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, the foci lie along the x-axis at:
- **First Focus:** (5, 0)
- **Second Focus:** (-5, 0)

**Final Answer:**

- **Asymptotes:**
  - \( y = \frac{4}{3}x \)
  - \( y = -\frac{4}{3}x \)

- **Foci:**
  - (5, 0) and (-5, 0)

Given the problem, we need to find the equation of a hyperbola with a transverse axis length of 6 units and vertices at (2, 3). 

First, we note that the transverse axis length is 6, so \(2a = 6\) which gives \(a = 3\).

We consider both possibilities for the direction of the hyperbola: horizontal and vertical.

1. **Horizontal Transverse Axis**:
   - The standard form of a horizontal hyperbola is \(\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1\).
   - Given the vertex at (2, 3), we know \(h + a = 2\) and \(k = 3\).
   - Solving for \(h\), we get \(h = 2 - 3 = -1\). Thus, the center is \((-1, 3)\).
   - The equation becomes \(\frac{(x + 1)^2}{9} - \frac{(y - 3)^2}{b^2} = 1\).

2. **Vertical Transverse Axis**:
   - The standard form of a vertical hyperbola is \(\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1\).
   - Given the vertex at (2, 3), we know \(k + a = 3\) and \(h = 2\).
   - Solving for \(k\), we get \(k = 3 - 3 = 0\). Thus, the center is \((2, 0)\).
   - The equation becomes \(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{(x - 2)^2}{b^2} = 1\).

Without additional information, both directions are possible. However, typically, problems assume a horizontal transverse axis unless specified otherwise. Therefore, we proceed with the horizontal case.

### Final Answer
The equation of the hyperbola is \boxed{\dfrac{(x + 1)^2}{9} - \dfrac{(y - 3)^2}{16} = 1}.

\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}

\[

- Comparing with the given equation, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \).

- The vertices are located at a distance of \( a \) from the center along the transverse axis (x-axis).
- Here, \( a^2 = 4 \), so \( a = 2 \).
- Therefore, the vertices are at (±2, 0).

- The foci are located at a distance of \( c \) from the center, where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \).
- Given \( a^2 = 4 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \), we find \( c^2 = 13 \) hence \( c = \sqrt{13} \).
- Thus, the foci are at (±√13, 0).

- For a horizontal hyperbola, the asymptotes are given by \( y = k ± \frac{b}{a}(x - h) \).
- Substituting the known values, we get \( y = ±\frac{3}{2}x \).

To determine the equation of the hyperbola and its asymptotes, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Standard Form**:  
   Since the hyperbola opens horizontally (transverse axis is horizontal), its standard form is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
2. **Determine Asymptotes**:  
   The asymptotes of a horizontally opening hyperbola are given by:
   \[
   y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)
   \]
   
3. **Apply Given Slopes**:  
   The slopes of the asymptotes are ±2, so:
   \[
   \frac{b}{a} = 2 \implies b = 2a
   \]
   
4. **Substitute \( b = 2a \) into the Standard Form**:  
   The equation becomes:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{(2a)^2} = 1
   \]
   
5. **Final Equation of the Hyperbola**:
   Simplify to express in terms of \( a \) and known constants (if provided).

6. **Asymptotes**:
   Using the slopes, the asymptotes are:
   \[
   y - k = \pm 2(x - h)
   \]

The final equation of the hyperbola is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{(x-h)^{2}}{a^{2}}-\frac{(y-k)^{2}}{4 a^{2}}=1}
\]
with asymptotes:
\[
\boxed{y = 2(x - h) + k \quad \text{and} \quad y = -2(x - h) + k}

So from earlier, we had equation: ((a -3)^2)/a^2 - 36/b^2=1

36/b² - 36/b²=1 ⇒0=1, which is impossible. So no solution here.

But when considering f(a,b), if we can approach negative infinity or positive depending on variables. Let's see:

If a and b both go to infinity, what happens? For example, take a approaches negative infinity while keeping a <b.

Let me fix a=-n where n>0 large, then choose b as some function of n such that a< b.

But f(a,b)= (-n)*b -2*(-n) +2b -4= -nb + 2n +2b -4

Group terms: ( -nb +2b ) + (2n -4 )

Factor out b: b(-n+2)+2(n-2)

So as n approaches infinity, if we set b to be fixed, say 0, then f(a,b)=0*(-n+2) +2(n-2)= approximately 2n which goes to infinity. So increasing.

Alternatively, take a=-n, b= k*n where k>1 (since a <b).

Then f(a,b)= (-n)(k n ) -2*(-n)+2(k n)-4

= -k n² +2n + 2kn -4

Now as n approaches infinity, the dominant term is -k n² which goes to negative infinity if k>0.
